			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Jumio built a real-time feature store on AWS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-jumio-built-a-real-time-feature-store-on-aws/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Amit Peshwani]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 18 Aug 2026 17:05:48 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">0ecd67497bc8d718139250b35b1ef3dd733d30e3</guid>

					<description>Learn how Jumio built a centralized, real-time feature store on AWS with Amazon SageMaker Feature Store, Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink, and Amazon Kinesis Data Streams. The architecture delivers sub-100ms feature serving for fraud detection and saves approximately $120,000 annually.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;If you’re managing a real-time feature store, you might be facing challenges such as data duplication, feature engineering, feature consistency, manual deployment, and latency. &lt;a href="https://www.jumio.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Jumio&lt;/a&gt; is an identity verification provider that helps businesses detect fraud and build digital trust. To provide these services in real time, Jumio’s machine learning (ML) models needed a real-time feature store that solves these challenges. We use Jumio’s case study to show you how to build a real-time feature store. This architecture pattern applies to ML use cases that require sub-100ms latency for real-time predictions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you the architecture, the design trade-offs, and their impact on Jumio’s workload. You learn how to optimize your ML feature management on AWS by using services such as &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/feature-store/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Feature Store&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/managed-service-apache-flink/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kinesis/data-streams/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Kinesis Data Streams&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="problem-statement"&gt;Problem statement&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before building the real-time feature store, feature engineering and deployment were often fragmented and inefficient. This led to the following issues:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data duplication&lt;/strong&gt;: Teams maintained their own offline feature stores, resulting in redundant data and inconsistent feature definitions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Manual production deployment&lt;/strong&gt;: Teams manually re-implemented features trained offline in production code (Java or Python), which increased the risk of mismatches and bugs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency challenges&lt;/strong&gt;: Fraud detection demands immediate access to features, including upstream model outputs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Delayed event handling&lt;/strong&gt;: Certain event types arrive with delays or in irregular patterns. They can appear shortly after initial activity or several weeks later because of extended review processes.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because Jumio’s business revolves around identity verification solutions, timely and accurate fraud detection is important. Jumio’s ML models rely heavily on features to make informed decisions. To address these challenges, Jumio needed a centralized, reusable, real-time feature store.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="technical-requirements"&gt;Technical requirements&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system’s feature store requirements span five interconnected dimensions that together define the platform.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The system must scale to accommodate high volumes of feature requests and a growing feature catalog, with the flexibility to evolve schemas over time without disrupting existing workflows.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To support the complexity of real-world ML use cases, it must offer feature engineering capabilities, including conditional feature creation and selection based on event times.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Low latency is a requirement particularly for fraud detection workflows, where features must be served in under 100 milliseconds.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model retraining and analysis require backfilling of historical data. To do this, the offline feature store ingests data in near real time for model retraining, debugging, evaluation, and monitoring.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Finally, the platform must support agile feature development by simplifying end-to-end feature development and deployment lifecycle, allowing cross-functional teams to independently introduce new features with minimal coordination overhead.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Jumio’s feature store architecture is a streaming-first design, built for scalability, reliability, and performance. We deployed this architecture in three AWS Regions: US East (N. Virginia) (us-east-1), Europe (Frankfurt) (eu-central-1), and Asia Pacific (Singapore) (ap-southeast-1). Here’s how data flows through the system:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Events enter through Kinesis.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Flink processes the events to features.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon SageMaker Feature Store stores features in-memory.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;ML models retrieve features for inference.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There is also a parallel data flow for the offline feature store. Events flow into Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) through Amazon Data Firehose (Firehose), then run through Amazon EMR and land as Iceberg tables for model training.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/05/ML-19991-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing three components: a data pipeline, a real-time feature store, and an offline feature store" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: The feature store architecture, which consists of a data pipeline, a real-time feature store, and an offline feature store&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-pipeline"&gt;Data pipeline&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Real-time ingestion flows through Amazon Kinesis Data Streams, where Apache Flink applications pick up incoming events. Flink processes and enriches the data in-flight before writing features directly to Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Batch processing takes a parallel path: Amazon Data Firehose delivers events to Amazon S3. An Amazon S3 event notification triggers Amazon EMR, which runs the heavier transformation workloads. The EMR process then populates the resulting processed features into Amazon SageMaker Feature Store (as cold data) and into an Apache Iceberg table, which serves as the offline feature store.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="real-time-and-offline-feature-store"&gt;Real-time and offline feature store&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture comprises both real-time and offline feature stores, each serving a distinct purpose.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Real-time feature store&lt;/strong&gt;: Jumio stores features in Amazon SageMaker Feature Store, optimized for low-latency model serving. 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hot data:&lt;/strong&gt; An in-memory store, powered by Amazon ElastiCache for Valkey, serves recent, frequently accessed features and supports low-latency reads and cost-effective writes.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cold data:&lt;/strong&gt; Less frequently accessed features are retained in the standard store for scalability and durability.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Offline feature store&lt;/strong&gt;: Flink output is routed to Amazon Data Firehose and then to Amazon S3, with data stored in Iceberg format. This makes features accessible from Amazon Athena, Amazon EMR interactive notebooks and jobs, and internal dataset preparation tools.Near real-time ingestion works as follows: 
  &lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;A Flink Sink writes to Amazon Data Firehose.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Firehose delivers to Amazon S3.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;An Amazon S3 event triggers AWS Lambda, which invokes Amazon EMR Serverless.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Amazon EMR Serverless updates the Iceberg tables.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="monitoring"&gt;Monitoring&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the real-time feature store, we focus on the latency and health of the streaming application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Records creation latency&lt;/strong&gt;: We monitor various stages within Flink applications, including:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Input Kinesis Data Streams to Flink consumer latency (milliseconds).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Flink consumer to Flink Sink latency (milliseconds).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Flink Sink to Amazon SageMaker Feature Store latency (milliseconds).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Latency from the input Kinesis Data Streams to Amazon SageMaker Feature Store (milliseconds).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink&lt;/strong&gt;: To verify that the Flink application is operating optimally, we track:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Busy time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Kinesis Processing Unit (KPU) usage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Last checkpoint duration, CPU, and memory utilization.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Backpressure time.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker Feature Store metrics&lt;/strong&gt;: We monitor the performance and reliability of the feature store:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;GET and PUT request volumes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Read and write latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Timeout rates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Record output size.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="offline-feature-store-monitoring"&gt;Offline feature store monitoring&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Data Firehose metrics&lt;/strong&gt;: Key metrics we monitor include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Incoming records (volume).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Put Records batch latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Records delivered to Amazon S3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delivery to Amazon S3 freshness.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delivery to Amazon S3 success.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="pros-and-cons-of-the-database-choice-framework-and-aws-services"&gt;Pros and cons of the database choice, framework, and AWS services&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We evaluated several approaches before settling on this architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-sagemaker-feature-store-database-choice"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Feature Store (database choice)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pros&lt;/strong&gt;: A managed service purpose-built for ML features reduces operational overhead, so your team can focus on model development instead of infrastructure management. It offers an in-memory store for low latency and a standard store for scalability.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cons&lt;/strong&gt;: Cost can be a factor without storage optimization.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="apache-flink-framework"&gt;Apache Flink (framework)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pros&lt;/strong&gt;: Powerful stream-processing capabilities that handle high data volumes, which keeps your fraud detection fast even during traffic spikes. It is also well suited for building features that represent complex event logic.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cons&lt;/strong&gt;: A higher learning curve compared to batch-processing frameworks, and the operational complexity of managing Flink applications.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="aws-services"&gt;AWS services&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pros&lt;/strong&gt;: A wide range of services (Amazon Kinesis, Amazon S3, Amazon Data Firehose, AWS Lambda, Amazon EMR Serverless, AWS Glue Data Catalog, and AWS Lake Formation), high scalability and reliability, and strong security features.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cons&lt;/strong&gt;: Integrating multiple services can be complex, and optimal configuration and cost management require deep AWS expertise.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="performance-metrics"&gt;Performance metrics&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Latency metrics showed a 95th-percentile response time of 16.9 milliseconds, which meets Jumio’s fraud detection SLA requirement of sub-100ms response times.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following image shows Jumio’s read latency, with a P50 (50th percentile) of 8.44 ms.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/05/ML-19991-2.png" alt="Graph showing Jumio’s read latency with a P50 of 8.44 ms" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Read latency, with a P50 of 8.44 ms&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following image shows Jumio’s write latency, with a P50 of 18.6 ms.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/05/ML-19991-3.png" alt="Graph showing Jumio’s write latency with a P50 of 18.6 ms" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Write latency, with a P50 of 18.6 ms&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="real-time-feature-store-records-overview"&gt;Real-time feature store records overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how we process late events.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/05/ML-19991-4.png" alt="Diagram showing how late-arriving events are processed and reconciled into the feature store records" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: How late-arriving events are processed&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="current-state-compared-to-previous-iterations"&gt;Current state compared to previous iterations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The current architecture represents a significant improvement over previous, fragmented approaches. Initially, individual teams across the organization defined features in a decentralized way. The current state has a centralized, reusable feature store. The deployment process is now automated and unified, replacing the manual implementation that took weeks. The system can now handle late-arriving features, as described earlier. This architecture provides real-time access through Amazon SageMaker Feature Store, whereas access to upstream models was previously limited. From a cost perspective, optimizing with the in-memory store in Amazon SageMaker Feature Store has saved approximately $120,000 in operational costs compared with the earlier disparate feature stores.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation-guidance"&gt;Implementation guidance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Through this feature store implementation and hard-won production lessons, we distilled the following guidance. A well-architected feature store rests on five principles. It starts with a &lt;em&gt;streaming-first design&lt;/em&gt; that makes features available to models in real time. &lt;em&gt;Centralized feature definitions&lt;/em&gt; support consistency and reusability across teams. A &lt;em&gt;tiered storage strategy&lt;/em&gt;, which pairs in-memory and standard stores, balances latency against cost. &lt;em&gt;Monitoring&lt;/em&gt; of feature store health and latency prevents silent degradation from corrupting model predictions. Underpinning this approach is &lt;em&gt;cross-functional collaboration&lt;/em&gt; between backend, ML, and data engineering teams, which keeps feature development moving from ideation to production without handoff friction.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="roi-and-impact"&gt;ROI and impact&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Key benefits include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Accelerated model development&lt;/strong&gt;: This approach can help reduce the time and effort required to develop new ML models by providing centralized, readily available features.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Improved model accuracy&lt;/strong&gt;: Consistent feature definitions and data access lead to more accurate ML models.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational cost savings&lt;/strong&gt;: Jumio achieved approximately $120K (based on its workload) in annual savings by migrating to Amazon SageMaker Feature Store without compromising latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enhanced customer experience&lt;/strong&gt;: Faster and more reliable identity verification processes contribute to a more secure customer experience.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Increased agility&lt;/strong&gt;: This flexible architecture supports iterating, deploying new features, and responding to evolving business needs.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you saw how Jumio built a real-time feature store on AWS that handles high-volume data ingestion, delivers millisecond latency, and saves approximately $120,000 annually. The feature store is a core component of Jumio’s continued innovation in AI-powered identity verification. The architecture and best practices outlined in this case study provide you a proven approach for use cases such as fraud detection, recommendations, and other ML use cases that require low-latency predictions. If you’ve faced similar challenges with feature stores, share your experiences and questions in the comments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, take the following next steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set up your Amazon Kinesis data stream to start collecting real-time data at scale for streaming data ingestion. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kinesis/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Kinesis&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink to process and transform your streaming data with minimal operational overhead. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/managed-service-apache-flink/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerate-ml-feature-pipelines-with-new-capabilities-in-amazon-sagemaker-feature-store/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Configure your feature repository&lt;/a&gt; to provide millisecond-level latency for real-time inference while maintaining offline storage for model training. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/feature-store/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Feature Store&lt;/a&gt; and its &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/07/amzn-sgm-feature-store-batch-write-list/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;latest updates&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

 &lt;p&gt;New to AWS? Explore these services using the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/free/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Free Tier&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

In his current role, he focuses on designing robust ML platform architectures. Outside of work, he enjoys traveling and watching movies.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Improve contract search accuracy with auto-generated filters in Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/improve-contract-search-accuracy-with-auto-generated-filters-in-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ariana Lopez]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 18 Aug 2026 17:02:52 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">7547030cee0c7ba1296ee9382d3859f784384ae1</guid>

					<description>In this post, we describe how AIDA works at a high level and how it helps address these challenges — grounding users in the right contracts, under the right legal context, and within the right access boundaries. Specifically, we explore how AIDA uses implicit and explicit filtering, along with metadata-enriched chunking in Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, to dramatically improve contract search accuracy.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Enterprises rely on large volumes of complex legal agreements to make critical business decisions — determining rights, renewal options, geographic restrictions, and compliance obligations. In industries like entertainment and media, where organizations manage thousands of contracts across multiple jurisdictions, this work remains largely manual: time-consuming, costly, and difficult to scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our AI-Driven Annotation (AIDA) solution, powered by AWS, helps address this challenge by transforming unstructured contracts into searchable, actionable intelligence. AIDA enables users to ask natural-language questions across large contract repositories — but delivering precise answers requires more than semantic search alone. Legal documents are highly contextual, and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG)-based systems can surface more content than a language model can effectively process. Without careful control over which excerpts are retrieved, and without sufficient document-level context, important clauses risk being overlooked or misinterpreted.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we describe how AIDA works at a high level and how it helps address these challenges — grounding users in the right contracts, under the right legal context, and within the right access boundaries. Specifically, we explore how AIDA uses implicit and explicit filtering, along with metadata-enriched chunking in Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, to dramatically improve contract search accuracy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137149" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/12/Screenshot-2026-08-11-at-11.45.38 PM.png" alt="" width="2046" height="820"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reference architecture diagram represents how AIDA uses a RAG architecture built on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt; to create an intelligent document query system. Data transmission between components uses encryption in transit via HTTPS/TLS 1.2+, including document uploads to Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, API calls to embedding models, vector database queries, and response delivery to client applications. For data stored in Amazon Bedrock, the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/shared-responsibility-model/"&gt;AWS shared responsibility model&lt;/a&gt; applies. The implementation follows a systematic workflow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="data-ingestion-flow"&gt;Data ingestion flow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1: Document ingestion and metadata configuration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture begins with document ingestion, where contracts are synced into Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases alongside structured metadata files. The metadata should contain key contract attributes such as parties, effective date, termination date, jurisdiction, and other key attributes that can enable powerful filtering capabilities downstream.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2: Chunking mechanism implementation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The knowledge base configuration phase implements a sophisticated chunking mechanism that breaks contracts into semantically meaningful segments optimized for retrieval. This chunking strategy helps provide each segment with sufficient context while remaining concise enough for efficient processing. The metadata supplied during ingestion plays a critical role in the system’s ability to apply intelligent filtering, particularly through the implicit filtering mechanism that distinguishes this architecture from standard RAG implementations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 3: Vector database storage&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once documents are ingested and processed, the system converts document chunks into vector embeddings and stores them in one of several vector database options available with Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, such as &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/"&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Service&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/features/vectors/"&gt;Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), both of which should be configured with encryption at rest enabled. This vector database serves as the foundation for semantic search, enabling the system to find conceptually similar content rather than relying solely on keyword matching.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Access to the knowledge base and model invocations are governed through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/?c=sc&amp;amp;sec=srvm&amp;amp;trk=7ad0b48b-3532-4cda-87b0-c63aef99e9cc&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; policies, with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/?nc2=type_a"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; logging enabled to maintain audit trails for compliance purposes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In terms of security, AIDA uses role-based access that is enforced using AWS IAM policies at the application layer in AIDA via project scoped roles, which restricts what each user can do.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="user-interaction-flow"&gt;User interaction flow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 4: Query embedding generation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a user submits a query, AIDA uses Amazon Bedrock Guardrails to help safeguard against prompt injections and data leaks, the system then follows an enhanced RAG workflow that begins by converting the query into embeddings using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/"&gt;Amazon Bedrock’s&lt;/a&gt; embedding models. This transformation enables semantic comparison between the user’s question and the stored document chunks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 5: Implicit and explicit filtering&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before performing semantic search, the system applies both implicit and explicit filtering mechanisms—a key innovation that significantly improves retrieval accuracy. The implicit filtering automatically applies metadata-based conditions, such as filtering by effective date ranges or specific contracting parties, before executing the vector search. This two-stage approach first narrows the search space through metadata constraints, then applies semantic similarity matching within that filtered subset, so that retrieved documents are both contextually relevant and meet specific business criteria.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 6: Semantic search and document retrieval&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The vector database retrieves the most relevant document chunks based on semantic similarity scores, typically using cosine similarity metrics to identify the closest matches to the user’s query. This semantic search operates on the filtered subset of documents, providing high precision and relevance in the results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 7: Prompt augmentation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The retrieved chunks are then used to augment the original user’s query in a carefully formatted prompt that combines the user’s question with relevant contract excerpts and metadata context. This augmented prompt provides the large language model (LLM) with the specific information required to generate a precise, grounded response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 8: LLM response generation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The augmented prompt is passed to a LLM through Amazon Bedrock, which generates a contextually precise response grounded in the actual contract documents rather than relying on the model’s training data alone. This approach helps provide responses that reflect the specific content of the organization’s contracts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition, AIDA uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; to apply content filtering, sensitive information (PII) safeguards, and prompt safety controls, to keep responses secure and aligned with enterprise and legal standards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 9: Response delivery with source attribution&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The final response is returned to the user through the Retrieve API, so that answers are not only relevant but also traceable back to specific source documents. This traceability reduces hallucinations, a common challenge with LLMs, and provides verifiable contract intelligence that users can trust for critical business decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="tuning-rag"&gt;Tuning RAG&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When developing a RAG system for legal contract analysis, one of the core challenges is managing the volume of potentially relevant information. Consider the following question:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“&lt;em&gt;Please identify any expired licensing agreements governed under California law? How do these agreements renew?&lt;/em&gt;”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Such a query might match hundreds of relevant text chunks across multiple documents. In RAG terminology, these excerpts are often referred to as candidate chunks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When Amazon Bedrock performs a semantic search to find the relevant document excerpts, it is designed to return the top k (max 100) relevant candidate chunks. The remaining candidate chunks that are filtered out could potentially contain critical context. Consequently, the LLM responses might be incomplete, overlook key insights, or even be inaccurate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Legal documents are highly structured and deeply dependent on context. In our example, a renewal clause in a California licensing agreement may operate differently from a renewal clause in a licensing agreement governed by another jurisdiction. If the retrieval step does not properly filter licensing agreements and California governing law, the system may return irrelevant contracts such as service agreements, NDAs, or agreements governed under other state laws. Even if those documents contain expiration or renewal language, they do not answer the original question. Effective metadata filtering becomes essential to narrow down the candidate pool to the most relevant subsets. This allows the subsequent LLM call to receive the context required to provide more precise and complete answers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It is important to note that while these retrieval improvements significantly enhance accuracy, AI-generated contract interpretations should always be reviewed by qualified legal professionals before being used for business decisions. AIDA is designed as a decision-support tool that can augment legal expertise rather than replacing it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two ways to assist in fine tuning the RAG are implicit filtering and explicit filtering which we will discuss next.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implicit-filtering"&gt;Implicit filtering&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Implicit filtering is a powerful capability in Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases that allows you to automatically filter search results based on metadata attributes without requiring explicit filter expressions in each query. This approach enables the system to pre-filter the vector store based on document metadata before performing semantic search, significantly narrowing the search space to the most relevant documents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The implicit filtering mechanism operates through a two-stage process:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stage 1: Metadata pre-filtering&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before executing the semantic similarity search, the system applies metadata-based conditions automatically. You can supply a custom metadata file (up to 10 KB per document) for each document in your knowledge base, containing attributes such as effective dates, document types, parties involved, or custom fields relevant to your use case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stage 2: Semantic search&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After narrowing the document set through metadata filters, the system performs vector similarity search only within this filtered subset. This helps reduce noise and irrelevant information while improving retrieval accuracy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="explicit-filtering"&gt;Explicit filtering&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Explicit filtering is applied consistently at the application layer, independent of user input. This approach helps provide search results that remain aligned with business policies, compliance requirements, and organizational constraints, rather than being left entirely to the interpretation of the retrieval engine. Explicit filters can therefore act as a safeguard, helping to verify that every query respects a critical boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Common use cases include the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Application-Level Constraints – Restricting retrieval results based on predefined system rules geographic restrictions – Designed to restrict users in certain regions only access documents aligned with local regulations (e.g., European users restricted to EU-only contracts).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Temporal Constraints – Returning only documents within a specific time frame (e.g., contracts active in the last two years).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Classification and Sensitivity – Restricting search results to only documents tagged with a required confidentiality or classification level.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Example of our explicit filtering:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "andAll": [
    { "listContains": { "key": "region", "value": "Germany" } },
    { "stringEquals": { "key": "confidentiality_level", "value": "Public" } }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="beyond-filtering-enriching-chunks-with-metadata-values"&gt;Beyond filtering: Enriching chunks with metadata values&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While implicit and explicit filtering narrows the pool of candidate chunks, they do not automatically provide the LLM with structured document-level context as described by the document metadata.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Metadata refers to structured attributes that describe a document beyond its raw text. These attributes are generated after document ingestion and indexing. Without these metadata values and context, the model may generate incomplete or less precise answers, especially in scenarios involving high volumes of contracts that are similar and complex in nature.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For legal contract analysis, common metadata fields include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Contract Type (e.g., Licensing Agreement)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Governing Law (e.g., California)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Effective Date&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Expiration Date&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Parties&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Business Unit&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confidentiality Level&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This structured layer enables filtering, policy enforcement, and richer reasoning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Let’s return to our guiding question:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“&lt;em&gt;Please identify any expired licensing agreements governed under California law? How do these agreements renew?&lt;/em&gt;”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Suppose one retrieved chunk contains the following clause:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“This Agreement shall automatically renew for successive one-year terms unless either party provides written notice of non-renewal at least 60 days prior to the expiration date.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If this clause is provided to the LLM without the document-level associated metadata values, the model cannot determine the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Whether the agreement is a licensing agreement.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Whether it is governed under California law.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Whether it has already expired.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The actual expiration date.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Without these metadata values, the model may generate incomplete or less accurate responses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To address this limitation, we enrich candidate chunks with document-level metadata values. Rather than duplicating metadata for every chunk, which would waste input tokens, we first group chunks by document and attach the relevant metadata once. This gives each chunk a richer context and helps the LLM reason more effectively, leading to more accurate, consistent, and reliable outputs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Finally, it’s important to recognize that the quality of improvements depends on the relevance and appropriateness of the metadata we choose. Filtering and enrichment work best when queries are aligned with the available metadata. Conversely, queries unrelated to those metadata values may not show the same benefits. Careful design and thoughtful selection of metadata fields are therefore essential to maximize the impact of this approach.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="evaluating-the-impact-of-filtering-and-enrichment"&gt;Evaluating the impact of filtering and enrichment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To evaluate the impact of filtering and metadata enrichment, we tested our guiding question:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“&lt;em&gt;Please identify any expired licensing agreements governed under California law? How do these agreements renew?&lt;/em&gt;”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The experiments were conducted on the &lt;a href="https://www.atticusprojectai.org/cuad" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Contract Understanding Atticus Dataset (CUAD)&lt;/a&gt; (licensing and co-branding agreements). For each test, the retrieval depth was set to top-k = 15. We compared four progressively enhanced configurations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Baseline RAG (no filters, no metadata).&lt;/strong&gt; In the baseline setup, retrieval relied purely on semantic similarity. The system retrieved 55 potential candidate clauses across documents. However, only a small portion of the relevant context made it into the top 15 results passed to the model.&lt;br&gt; Observations:&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The model correctly identified one expired agreement (Snap/United).&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Renewal terms were partially described.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The response lacked confidence and full contextual grounding.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Explicit filtering only.&lt;/strong&gt; Next, we applied explicit filters (for example, restricting results to U.S. contracts). This reduced the candidate pool, but accuracy did not improve consistently.&lt;br&gt; Observations:&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;One agreement was incorrectly classified as expired.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Renewal clauses were misinterpreted.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Filtering alone narrowed the search space but did not provide enough structured context for precise reasoning.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Implicit and explicit filtering.&lt;/strong&gt; We then combined query-derived filters (California law) and application-level constraints (U.S. contracts). This reduced the number of competing documents and improved contextual focus.&lt;br&gt; Observations:&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The model identified the correct agreement.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;However, it hesitated to definitively classify it as expired.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Renewal mechanisms were described but cautiously phrased.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Filtering improved retrieval precision, but uncertainty remained.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Filtering and metadata enrichment.&lt;/strong&gt; Finally, we enriched retrieved chunks with document-level metadata such as governing law, expiration date, and contract type.&lt;br&gt; Observations:&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The system correctly identified only Snap/United as expired.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Renewal terms were clearly and accurately explained.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The response was grounded in both clause text and structured attributes.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;This configuration produced the most accurate and confident output.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;table style="height: 621px" border="1px" width="1026" cellpadding="5px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Setup&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Candidate pool (n)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Coverage (15/k)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expired agreements identified&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Renewal terms explained&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

   &lt;td width="104"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;75.0%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

   &lt;td width="104"&gt;Mixed – hedged on expiration&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

   &lt;td width="104"&gt;20 (+ metadata)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;75.0%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;&amp;nbsp;Correctly identified only &lt;em&gt;Snap/United&lt;/em&gt; as expired&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;Renewal terms clearly explained for all others&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td width="104"&gt;Most reliable – precise and clause-grounded&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed you how AIDA helps customers evaluate legal documents through the use of AI. Specifically, we described how AIDA utilizes explicit and implicit filtering as well as enriching chunks with metadata values to improve the results from RAG, increasing accuracy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By embedding these controls directly into the retrieval pipeline, AIDA can provide users with insights derived from contracts they are authorized to access, while preserving the legal and business context required for confident decision-making. The result is a practical, enterprise-ready approach to contract intelligence reducing manual review effort, improving answer quality, and helping organizations unlock the overall value of their contractual data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/knowledge-base.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases documentation&lt;/a&gt;, and explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; in the AWS Management Console.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Axonius built secure multi-tenant AI agents on Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-axonius-built-secure-multi-tenant-ai-agents-on-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Amir Krispin]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 18 Aug 2026 16:27:07 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">a1f4ace6ed8c5df4ecb9d93eabb3007247912d1f</guid>

					<description>Learn how Axonius, a cybersecurity SaaS provider, used Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to deploy fully isolated, multi-tenant AI agents across hundreds of customer environments, without building custom compute isolation, authentication, or observability infrastructure from scratch.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Independent Software Vendors (ISVs) are expanding their offerings and adding AI agents. Common considerations for organizations when adding agentic workloads include security, scalability, time to market, and cost tracking. ISVs have another dimension, they provide services to other organizations and need to manage agentic workloads for each customer. As such, ISVs need to manage those common challenges not only broadly but at the tenant level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.axonius.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Axonius&lt;/a&gt; is the asset intelligence platform that helps Security and IT teams prioritize risks and coordinate fixes. By reconciling data from over 1,400 systems into one authoritative source of truth, Axonius enables security teams and the teams they support to collaborate effectively, reducing the manual burden of security, audit, and compliance by up to 50%. Axonius runs its software as a service (SaaS) infrastructure on AWS, managing hundreds of isolated customer environments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. In this post, we cover strategies available for SaaS providers to deploy AI agents, describe how Amazon Bedrock AgentCore supports those options, look into Axonius’s considerations, and share Axonius’s chosen architecture. We also describe how Axonius integrated agentic workloads with its existing methodology. This post is for platform engineers and architects building secure, multi-tenant AI agent deployments on AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="multi-tenancy-patterns-for-ai-agents"&gt;Multi-tenancy patterns for AI agents&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Independent Software Vendors (ISVs) tend to provide services using the SaaS model. When choosing an architecture, ISVs have &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/saas-lens/silo-pool-and-bridge-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;three common architectural patterns: silo, bridge, and pool.&lt;/a&gt; The following sections elaborate on what each model means when we look into agentic AI with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;em&gt;silo model&lt;/em&gt; refers to an architecture where tenants are provided dedicated resources. Looking into AgentCore runtime, it means using a dedicated agent per tenant. With the &lt;em&gt;pool model&lt;/em&gt;, tenants share resources, one agent serves multiple tenants. With AgentCore runtim, you can &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-sessions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;isolate each user session&lt;/a&gt; by allocating a unique session ID for each user session. The third pattern is the &lt;em&gt;bridge model&lt;/em&gt; in which some components are in silo mode while others are in pool mode. For example, we can have a dedicated agent deployed into AgentCore runtime using shared &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;, an AWS managed Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge"&gt;The challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Axonius was looking to add AI agents to their offering. Their first AI agent interprets the state of large enterprise environments and identifies gaps and risks, using AI to analyze and make sense of millions of data points from dozens of concurrent integration sources. This initiative allows junior analysts to run complex analyses without tying up senior analysts in hours of manual work. Axonius opted to maintain their current tenant management methodology.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Axonius’s SaaS deployment model is silo. Each customer workload resides in a dedicated Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC). The VPC contains an Application Load Balancer (ALB), Network Load Balancer (NLB), databases, and general compute infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Axonius wanted to introduce AI agents while keeping a silo deployment model. Axonius needed to address several key requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tenant isolation –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Axonius handles sensitive customer data. An agent serving one customer must be scoped exclusively to that customer’s data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The existing service has an authentication and authorization module in place, residing on the Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) allocated to the tenant. Axonius needed to integrate the agent’s identity flow with the existing module without disruption.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost tracking –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Agentic costs can spiral. Most of the cost is in model invocation, thus it’s critical to track model cost per tenant. Knowing your cost is essential when considering pricing options for agentic offerings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Integration with the service&lt;/strong&gt; – The agent instance associated with a tenant needs to have secure access to that tenant workload’s APIs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Lifecycle management&lt;/strong&gt; – Adding the agentic workload to the current silo Continuous Delivery (CD) workflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observability –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;With a silo model, Axonius will have a large number of agents. The DevOps team needed high quality, straightforward to integrate observability that can track large fleets of agents, issue alarms when things go wrong, and provide tracing capabilities to debug issues.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections cover the options Axonius evaluated and what they chose.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="possible-solutions"&gt;Possible solutions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Looking into tenant deployment modes, Axonius considered the following options.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-1-pool-shared-runtime-with-jwt-based-tenant-routing"&gt;Option 1: Pool — shared runtime with JWT-based tenant routing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore runtime enforces structural separation by allocating a dedicated microVM for each session. A single runtime serves tenants while maintaining full tenant separation through application-level controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tenants authenticate through an OAuth 2.0 identity provider (for example, Amazon Cognito) with JWTs carrying a unique tenant claim (for example, custom:tenant_id). The runtime’s &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/inbound-jwt-authorizer.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;built-in JWT authorizer&lt;/a&gt; uses the configured discovery URL to fetch public keys and validate the token’s issuer. The agent code then reads the claim to route tool calls and data access to the correct tenant environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136732" style="width: 4351px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136732" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136732" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/multi-tanant-gateway.drawio.png" alt="" width="4341" height="1236"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136732" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Pool model with a single shared runtime and JWT-based tenant routing&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="advantages"&gt;Advantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational simplicity&lt;/strong&gt;: Only one runtime to deploy, monitor, and update.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Rapid onboarding&lt;/strong&gt;: New customers onboard immediately without infrastructure provisioning, reducing time-to-value.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="disadvantages"&gt;Disadvantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Application-dependent isolation&lt;/strong&gt;: Tenant separation relies entirely on application code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Homogeneous deployment&lt;/strong&gt;: Per-tenant customization requires additional conditional logic.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-2-bridge-shared-runtime-plus-gateway-enforced-tool-isolation"&gt;Option 2: Bridge — shared runtime plus gateway-enforced tool isolation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A hybrid approach combines the operational simplicity of a single shared Runtime with infrastructure-level tenant enforcement at the tool layer through AgentCore Gateway. Tenants share one Runtime, but every outbound tool call passes through a Gateway that enforces tenant boundaries before the tool code executes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="how-it-works-1"&gt;How it works&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tenants connect to the same AgentCore runtime, which uses dedicated microVMs per session for compute isolation, identical to Option 1. The difference is that every tool invocation routes through a shared AgentCore Gateway with two enforcement mechanisms:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt; – Deterministic access control. Cedar rules evaluate each tool call against the caller’s identity attributes (for example, Cognito group claims) and produce an allow/deny decision. A forbid rule can block specific tenant groups from invoking restricted tools.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda Interceptor (REQUEST)&lt;/strong&gt; – Dynamic validation and context enrichment. Runs before the tool call reaches the target extracts the JWT, looks up tenant context, and exchanges the token for short-lived, tenant-scoped IAM credentials through STS AssumeRole (“act-on-behalf” pattern). The downstream tool receives these least-privilege credentials instead of the original JWT.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway evaluates the interceptor &lt;em&gt;before&lt;/em&gt; the Cedar policy, enabling the interceptor to enrich the request context that the policy then evaluates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A RESPONSE interceptor can additionally filter tool discovery based on tenant identity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This means two independent mechanisms enforce tenant isolation at the infrastructure layer, outside agent code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136733" style="width: 3397px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136733" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136733" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/multi-tanant-slio.drawio.png" alt="" width="3387" height="1236"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136733" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Bridge model with a shared runtime and gateway-enforced tool isolation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="advantages-1"&gt;Advantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layered infrastructure enforcement:&lt;/strong&gt; Even if the agent code has a routing bug, the Gateway blocks cross-tenant tool calls. Cedar policy (deterministic) and a Lambda interceptor (dynamic) provide defense in depth.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Centralized governance&lt;/strong&gt;: A single Gateway to audit and enforce tenant policies across all tool invocations, with every decision logged to Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Shared efficiency:&lt;/strong&gt; Runtime, knowledge bases, and observability infrastructure are managed centrally without sharing security risk.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="disadvantages-1"&gt;Disadvantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Setup complexity:&lt;/strong&gt; Requires configuring a Gateway, REQUEST/RESPONSE interceptor Lambdas, tenant mappings, Cedar policies, and STS role trust relationships. This adds significantly more moving parts than Option 1 or 3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;VPC connectivity&lt;/strong&gt;: Backend tools typically reside in a VPC, requiring VPC endpoints for private Gateway connectivity, which adds networking complexity and potential points of failure.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-3-silo-dedicated-runtime-per-tenant"&gt;Option 3: Silo — dedicated runtime per tenant&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the silo model, every tenant operates on a dedicated AgentCore runtime. Access control is enforced entirely through AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM). Resource-based policies on both the Runtime and its endpoint determine which principals, whether same-account roles or cross-account identities, are allowed to invoke the agent. Because each tenant’s workload runs on separate infrastructure, there is no shared compute between tenants.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136735" style="width: 3610px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136735" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136735" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/multi-tantns-microVM-session.drawio.png" alt="" width="3600" height="936"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136735" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Silo model with a dedicated runtime per tenant&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="how-it-works-2"&gt;How it works&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each tenant gets a dedicated AgentCore runtime, provisioned automatically (for example, with AWS CloudFormation or CDK) when a new customer is onboarded. Within that runtime, each user session runs in its own isolated microVM, so no process state is shared even between users of the same tenant. Access control is enforced through IAM resource-based policies attached to both the AgentCore runtime and its endpoint. The caller must hold explicit permission for their tenant’s specific runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="advantages-2"&gt;Advantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Maximum isolation:&lt;/strong&gt; Dedicated compute per tenant with no shared process state. Each session runs in its own microVM, and tenants cannot reach each other’s infrastructure.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Simple authorization model:&lt;/strong&gt; IAM resource-based policies, applied to both the Runtime and its endpoint, are the sole enforcement point. No application-level routing logic required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Independent configuration:&lt;/strong&gt; Each runtime can run a different agent version, model, or endpoint configuration without affecting other tenants.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="disadvantages-2"&gt;Disadvantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scale limitations:&lt;/strong&gt; The default quota is 1,000 agents per AWS account (adjustable through Service Quotas), which requires capacity planning for large customer bases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Provisioning latency:&lt;/strong&gt; Each new tenant requires creating a dedicated Runtime and endpoint, introducing onboarding delays compared to a shared-runtime model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational overhead:&lt;/strong&gt; Monitoring, updating, and managing hundreds of runtimes adds operational complexity that demands robust automation (for example, CDK/CloudFormation pipelines, centralized observability).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Axonius operates today in a siloed mode and chooses to continue with that approach when adding agents. Each customer has a dedicated agent. Axonius designed a multi-tenant agentic AI architecture using Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore with the following key components:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore runtime&lt;/strong&gt; – Axonius deploys a dedicated agent for each customer, and each user session runs on an isolated microVM, using AgentCore runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)&lt;/strong&gt; – Stores per-tenant agent container images.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/strong&gt; – Powers the underlying foundation models (FMs), with IAM role tagging for cost allocation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases (KB)&lt;/strong&gt; – Axonius uses Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases (KB) with Amazon S3 Vectors because of their cost efficiency and ability to scale. Axonius uses metadata filtering to isolate tenant-specific data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/strong&gt; – Provides content filtering and topic denial policies. Guardrails are applied to every model response, keeping responses safe and in scope before they are shared with the user.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/strong&gt; – Monitors the overall workload and is instrumental in cost control. It tracks token consumption metrics (input/output), issuing alerts and using IAM-deny enforcement for cost governance. If a customer exceeds their token budget, an automated IAM policy blocks further invocations.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon VPC Lattice&lt;/strong&gt; –&amp;nbsp;Allows Axonius to manage cost-efficient private connectivity, connecting AgentCore runtime, customer VPCs, and AWS service endpoints.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture uses AWS CloudFormation for automated per-customer provisioning and teardown, allowing Axonius to scale agent deployments across their customer base.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_137132" style="width: 2570px" class="wp-caption alignleft"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-137132" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-137132" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/11/AgentCore-HLD-Copy-of-AgentCore.jpeg" alt="" width="2560" height="2089"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-137132" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Figure 4: Axonius multi-tenant agentic architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-runtime"&gt;Why Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After evaluating multiple approaches, including running agents as additional containers within their existing EC2 instances, Axonius chose Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime. Its purpose-built capabilities directly addressed their multi-tenant SaaS requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Session isolation with microVMs&lt;/strong&gt; – AgentCore runtime’s session isolation was the decisive factor. Each user session runs in a &lt;em&gt;dedicated microVM&lt;/em&gt; with isolated CPU, memory, and filesystem resources. After session completion, the entire microVM is terminated and memory is sanitized. This deterministic security model was critical for Axonius, where each customer’s data, including sensitive cybersecurity asset inventories, must remain completely isolated.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Framework flexibility and VPC integration&lt;/strong&gt; – AgentCore runtime’s framework-agnostic design allowed Axonius to deploy agents using their preferred tooling while connecting securely to their existing VPC infrastructure. Each agent connects through an ENI to the customer’s VPC, allowing direct interaction with the Axonius application APIs running inside the isolated EC2 instances.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Built-in observability&lt;/strong&gt; – The built-in observability capabilities gave Axonius the visibility they needed without building custom monitoring infrastructure. These capabilities include CloudWatch integration for logging, AWS X-Ray for distributed tracing, and agent-specific tracing that captures reasoning steps and tool invocations.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="user-session-flow"&gt;User session flow&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each customer runs their own Axonius deployment on a dedicated workload inside their own VPC. Every customer also gets a dedicated AgentCore runtime: a per-customer agent that reasons with Claude, pulls product knowledge from an Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Base, and reaches back into that customer’s Axonius API to answer data questions. Customers have a chat box that allows them to write questions. The following flow traces a customer question, for example, &lt;em&gt;“&lt;em&gt;Are there any major changes in my asset count compared to last week?&lt;/em&gt;”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authentication:&lt;/strong&gt; The user is already signed in to Axonius on their own instance. When they send a chat message, the Axonius application authenticates the request and mints a short-lived impersonation JWT carrying the user’s identity, tenant ID, session ID, and actor ID. This token, rather than a static credential, authorizes everything downstream.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Runtime invocation&lt;/strong&gt;: The Axonius application control plane assembles the invocation payload and calls InvokeAgentRuntime on the customer’s dedicated AgentCore runtime. The payload carries only tenant configuration: the AgentCore Memory ID, Knowledge Base and data-source IDs, AWS Region, current Axonius version, and the callback address for the customer’s own instance. The JWT rides in a custom AgentCore header rather than in the body, so authentication material does not land in the agent’s saved state, and the session and actor IDs scope the call to one user’s conversation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dedicated, isolated runtime&lt;/strong&gt;: Each customer has its own AgentCore runtime, and that runtime is attached to a dedicated elastic network interface (ENI) placed inside the customer’s own VPC and subnet. AgentCore creates an isolated session, validates the JWT back against the customer’s Axonius application (signature, expiry, revocation, and permissions), and scopes the reasoning to that single tenant. An invalid token is rejected before the model runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent reasoning with Claude&lt;/strong&gt;: The team’s existing LangGraph supervisor inspects the question and routes it to the right specialist agent. The specialist uses Amazon Bedrock to analyze the question, deciding at each step whether it can respond directly, needs product documentation, or needs live data from the customer’s environment. For model availability by Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region&lt;/a&gt; in Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Knowledge retrieval:&lt;/strong&gt; When the question is about how Axonius works, the agent retrieves relevant passages from a Bedrock Knowledge Base. That knowledge base is kept current by uploading the latest Axonius documentation to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and syncing it, so responses reflect the customer’s running version.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool access into the customer’s Axonius API&lt;/strong&gt;: When the question needs live data, such as “Give me a high-level summary of all assets discovered in the last 24 hours,” the agent invokes its query tool, which: 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Uses the Knowledge Base and large language model (LLM) to translate the natural-language question into an Axonius Query Language (AQL) expression.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Reaches the customer’s Axonius application instance through the runtime’s dedicated ENI, so the traffic stays inside the customer’s VPC and doesn’t leave the AWS network. It compiles the query and fetches the matching assets, carrying the same JWT, so the agent can only ever see data the user is already allowed to see.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Returns the results to the agent, which reasons over them to compose the response.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Guardrails and response&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Bedrock Guardrails are applied server-side to every model response, keeping responses safe and on scope before they leave Amazon Bedrock. The agent’s answer streams back token by token through the runtime to the chat UI, and the exchange is persisted in AgentCore Memory so follow-up questions keep context.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="tenant-isolation-and-private-networking"&gt;Tenant isolation and private networking&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The whole design rests on strict per-customer isolation and private connectivity:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dedicated runtime per customer:&lt;/strong&gt; Every customer gets its own AgentCore runtime with its own ENI inside their VPC and subnet, so one customer’s agent has no network path to another customer’s data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In-network access to customer data:&lt;/strong&gt; Because the ENI lives in the customer’s VPC, the runtime reaches the internal Axonius EC2 instance over private addressing. That traffic doesn’t traverse the public internet and does not leave the AWS network.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Shared AWS services over Amazon VPC Lattice:&lt;/strong&gt; A dedicated Axonius services VPC publishes the AWS services the runtime depends on (Amazon ECR, Amazon S3, Amazon Bedrock, and others) through a set of private endpoints, shared with each customer VPC through VPC Lattice. This lets the AgentCore runtime pull the agent container image from Amazon ECR and reach Amazon Bedrock and the Knowledge Base privately, while restricting access so that only the customer VPC and its runtime can reach those services.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Networking cost efficiency:&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon VPC Lattice allows Axonius to define one private endpoint per AWS service, reducing cost and management overhead. With AWS PrivateLink, Axonius would have needed to define a private endpoint per VPC per service.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together, this keeps a clean trust boundary: the reasoning, knowledge retrieval, and guardrails run on Amazon Bedrock. The authoritative data and identity stay on each customer’s own VPC. And every hop between them travels private AWS networking. The agent does not hold long-lived credentials. It borrows the user’s JWT for the life of a request, and every session, memory, tool call, and network path is scoped to a single tenant.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="token-governance-and-cost-management"&gt;Token governance and cost management&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A notable aspect of Axonius’s implementation is their approach to token governance:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CloudWatch metrics&lt;/strong&gt; track sum of input and output tokens per agent. In addition, Axonius uses opentelemetry-instrument to get real-time token usage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;IAM role tagging&lt;/strong&gt; (using Amazon Bedrock cost allocation by IAM user/role feature) allows per-tenant cost attribution.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automated IAM-deny policies&lt;/strong&gt; can be triggered by using CloudWatch alarms to block runaway agent usage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Application Inference Profiles&lt;/strong&gt; per model allow granular tagging, alerting, and cost control through Amazon EventBridge.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This approach provides cost allocation visibility updated once or twice daily, with real-time alerting through CloudWatch for immediate enforcement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ISVs are in the process of developing agentic AI workloads. This post describes possible avenues and Axonius’s chosen solution, using Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore as the foundation for its agentic AI offering.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By using the AgentCore managed runtime, Knowledge Bases for RAG, and built-in session memory, Axonius reduced the development cycle for their multi-tenant AI agent from an estimated eight weeks of custom infrastructure work to only 10 days of production-ready deployment, a 75 percent reduction in time-to-market.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“AgentCore Runtime gave us the multi-tenant isolation and authentication framework we needed to deploy AI agents across large numbers of customer environments without compromising our security-first architecture.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Dori Shmuel, SaaS DevOps Lead, Axonius&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about building multi-tenant AI agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, we recommend you review these additional resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://catalog.us-east-1.prod.workshops.aws/workshops/749d2432-98e2-4af5-b8d8-7242395c1925/en-US" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting Started with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Workshop&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-configure.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability Configuration&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

		<title>NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning now available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/nvidia-nemotron-3-5-lightning-now-available-in-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Venu Kanamatareddy]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 17 Aug 2026 18:06:33 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
