			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Part 2: Amazon Bedrock cost attribution with Amazon Athena and CUDOS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/part-2-amazon-bedrock-cost-attribution-with-amazon-athena-and-cudos/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Abhi Shivaditya]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 12 Aug 2026 17:45:20 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Athena]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">bdab25a99afb30707f36430d746dc6f3704a1935</guid>

					<description>Learn how to visualize and analyze Amazon Bedrock cost attribution using Amazon Athena and CUDOS dashboards. This post shows how to set up CUR 2.0 with IAM principal data, query Bedrock spend by principal, project, and team, and build dashboards to track AI costs across your organization.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-granular-cost-attribution-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; introduced granular cost attribution for Amazon Bedrock. This feature automatically traces every inference request back to the IAM principal that made the call. It showed how the new &lt;code&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/code&gt; column can give you per-user and per-application visibility. With optional cost allocation tags, you can also aggregate spend by team, project, or tenant using AWS Cost Explorer. With this approach, you can typically track usage at the granularity you want for any Bedrock-powered service or application, whether third-party tools like Claude Code or Codex or your own custom builds. This post shows how to visualize and analyze cost attribution for Amazon Bedrock through Amazon Athena queries and CUDOS dashboards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, this post shows you how to set up Cost and Usage Report (CUR) 2.0 through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cur/latest/userguide/dataexports-create.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Data Exports&lt;/a&gt; instrumented with IAM principal data. Next, this post shows you how to query CUR data with Amazon Athena for analysis. It then introduces the new capabilities of CUDOS dashboards, including granular Bedrock cost and usage data. While Athena provides flexibility for aggregations, integration with different business intelligence (BI) tools, and chargeback processes, CUDOS offers pre-built visuals tailored to your organization’s specific structure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-21078-1.png" alt="Table of example query output with columns for IAM principal, usage type, and unblended cost, showing per-user Claude model token costs" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Example query output showing Amazon Bedrock costs by IAM principal and usage type&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="setting-up-cost-and-usage-reports-cur-2.0"&gt;Setting up Cost and Usage Reports (CUR 2.0)&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you can analyze Amazon Bedrock costs, set up a CUR 2.0 data export and connect it to Amazon Athena.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with billing console access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;IAM permissions for Cost and Usage Reports, S3, and Athena.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An S3 bucket for CUR data storage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Basic familiarity with SQL and the AWS Management Console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;(Optional) Claude Code or Kiro-CLI for automated setup.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Make sure you have the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-cur-iam-principal-bedrock-tracking/tree/main#required-iam-permissions" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;required IAM permissions&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Amazon Bedrock cost attribution, you need to enable IAM principal data in your CUR 2.0 export so that the &lt;code&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/code&gt; column and associated IAM principal tags are populated.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="creating-a-cur-2.0-data-export-with-iam-principal-data"&gt;Creating a CUR 2.0 data export with IAM principal data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow the instructions in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cur/latest/userguide/dataexports-create-standard.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Creating a standard data export&lt;/a&gt; to set up your CUR 2.0 export. When configuring the export, make sure the following options are part of your final configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-21078-2.png" alt="The Create export page in the AWS Billing and Cost Management console with Standard data export, CUR 2.0, and Include caller identity (IAM principal) allocation data selected" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Creating a CUR 2.0 standard data export with caller identity (IAM principal) allocation data enabled&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In &lt;strong&gt;Additional export content&lt;/strong&gt;, select the following checkboxes: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Include caller identity (IAM principal) allocation data&lt;/strong&gt; — This is the critical setting that populates the &lt;code&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/code&gt; column and surfaces IAM principal tags (prefixed with &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/&lt;/code&gt;) in your cost data.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In &lt;strong&gt;Data table configurations&lt;/strong&gt;: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time granularity&lt;/strong&gt;: Select Hourly for maximum detail.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In &lt;strong&gt;Data export delivery options&lt;/strong&gt;: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;File versioning&lt;/strong&gt;: Select Overwrite existing report to avoid storing duplicate data.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Important:&lt;/strong&gt; Enabling IAM principal data increases CUR file sizes because usage that was previously a single row is now expanded into multiple rows, one for each IAM principal that contributed to the usage. For high-volume workloads with many distinct principals, plan your Amazon S3 storage accordingly and consider &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lifecycle-mgmt.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 Lifecycle policies&lt;/a&gt; for older CUR files.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It may take up to 24 hours for AWS to deliver your first CUR 2.0 report to the S3 bucket.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="connecting-cur-2.0-to-amazon-athena"&gt;Connecting CUR 2.0 to Amazon Athena&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Amazon Athena, you can query your CUR data using standard SQL with no infrastructure to manage. To streamline this setup, an optional agent.md skill repo is available that you can use with AI assistants like Claude Code, Kiro-CLI, or Codex. It automates the end-to-end process of connecting your Athena environment to your CUR data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the repo (&lt;code&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-cur-iam-principal-bedrock-tracking&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Launch Claude Code (&lt;code&gt;claude&lt;/code&gt;) or Kiro CLI (&lt;code&gt;kiro&lt;/code&gt;) in this directory.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Prompt: “Read agent.md and follow its workflow to set up Cost and Usage Report tracking and run the Amazon Bedrock-by-principal query for the current month.”&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You may also follow the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-cur-iam-principal-bedrock-tracking/blob/main/manual_setup.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;manual setup steps&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you plan to deploy the CUDOS dashboard as well, you can &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/guidance/latest/cloud-intelligence-dashboards/deployment-in-global-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;deploy it from AWS CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt;. This also deploys the Athena query database as part of the process.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once deployed, here is a sample test query in the &lt;a href="https://us-east-1.console.aws.amazon.com/athena/home?region=us-east-1#/query-editor/history" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Athena Query Editor&lt;/a&gt; that you can use:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
    line_item_iam_principal,
    line_item_usage_type,
    line_item_unblended_cost
    --# Note: replace your_cur_table_name, like `cid_data_export.cur2`
FROM your_cur_table_name
WHERE line_item_product_code in ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
    AND line_item_iam_principal IS NOT NULL
LIMIT 10;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If this returns rows with IAM principal ARNs and Bedrock usage types, your setup is complete and ready for deeper analysis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="athena-query-patterns-for-bedrock-cost-tracking"&gt;Athena query patterns for Bedrock cost tracking&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With your CUR 2.0 data available in Athena, you can now answer granular cost attribution questions using SQL. This section provides three progressively sophisticated query patterns that cover the most common analysis scenarios.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; In the following queries, replace &lt;code&gt;your_cur_table_name&lt;/code&gt; with the actual name of your CUR Athena table (for example, &lt;code&gt;cid_data_export.cur2&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-1-bedrock-costs-by-iam-principal-and-usage-type"&gt;Query 1: Bedrock costs by IAM principal and usage type&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This query provides a complete breakdown of Amazon Bedrock spending by caller identity and model usage. It answers the question: &lt;em&gt;“Who is calling which models, and how much are they spending?”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
    line_item_iam_principal,
    line_item_usage_type,
    SUM(line_item_usage_amount) AS total_tokens,
    SUM(line_item_unblended_cost) AS total_cost
FROM your_cur_table_name
WHERE line_item_product_code in ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
    AND billing_period = DATE_FORMAT(CURRENT_DATE, '%Y-%m')
    AND line_item_iam_principal IS NOT NULL
    -- AND line_item_usage_type LIKE '%Sonnet%input%'
GROUP BY
    line_item_iam_principal,
    line_item_usage_type
ORDER BY total_cost DESC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example output:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;line_item_usage_type&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;total_tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;total_cost&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/ChatApp/session-1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USW2-anthropic.claude-opus-4-8-mantle-cache-write-tokens-standard&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1629.5&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$11.2029&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/DocProcessor/batch-7&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USW2-Claude4.6Sonnet-output-tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;68.579&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$1.131&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/ClaudeCode/chat&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USW2-Claude4.6Sonnet-cache-write-input-token-count&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;831.74&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$3.4309&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/alice&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USW2-Claude4.6Sonnet-input-tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;17.33&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0.0572&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Analysis tips:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use LIKE patterns such as &lt;code&gt;line_item_usage_type LIKE '%Sonnet%output%'&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;%nova%&lt;/code&gt; to filter for specific models.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;code&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/code&gt; column contains the full ARN. For assumed roles, the session name after the last &lt;code&gt;/&lt;/code&gt; identifies the specific user or session.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-2-costs-broken-down-by-known-iam-principal-tags"&gt;Query 2: Costs broken down by known IAM principal tags&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you’ve tagged your IAM principals with dimensions like &lt;code&gt;team&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;project&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;costcenter&lt;/code&gt; (and activated those tags as cost allocation tags), they appear in your CUR 2.0 data as part of the &lt;em&gt;tags&lt;/em&gt; columns, with the &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/&lt;/code&gt; prefix followed by your tag key. This query groups costs by those tags to answer questions like: &lt;em&gt;“How much did the engineering team spend on Bedrock this month?”&lt;/em&gt; or &lt;em&gt;“What’s the total Bedrock cost for the chatbot project?”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;By project:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
    tags['iamPrincipal/project'] AS project,
    line_item_usage_type,
    SUM(line_item_usage_amount) AS total_tokens,
    SUM(line_item_unblended_cost) AS total_cost
FROM your_cur_table_name
WHERE line_item_product_code in ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
    AND billing_period = DATE_FORMAT(CURRENT_DATE, '%Y-%m')
    AND line_item_iam_principal IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY
    tags['iamPrincipal/project'],
    line_item_usage_type
ORDER BY total_cost DESC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example output:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;project&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;line_item_usage_type&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;total_tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;total_cost&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;data-science&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USW2-Claude4.5Sonnet-cache-write-input-token-count&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;433.893&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1.789808625&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;data-science&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USW2-Claude4.6Sonnet-cache-read-input-token-count&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5372.659&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1.77297747&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;engineering&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USW2-Claude4.5Sonnet-input-tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;29.481&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.0972873&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;engineering&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USW2-Claude4.5Sonnet-output-tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;31.102&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.513183&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; This query returns results only if your IAM principals have been tagged with the relevant keys and those tags have been activated as cost allocation tags.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-3-dynamic-tag-discovery-using-unnest-for-unknown-tag-schemas"&gt;Query 3: Dynamic tag discovery using UNNEST for unknown tag schemas&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In large organizations, you may not know in advance which tags have been applied across all IAM principals. Different teams might use different tag keys, or new tags might be introduced over time. The following example shows how to dynamically explore using Athena’s UNNEST function.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This query discovers all IAM principal tags being used across your Bedrock workloads and shows cost allocation for each tag key-value pair:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;WITH iam_principal_costs AS (
    SELECT
        t.key AS tag_name,
        t.value AS tag_value,
        line_item_usage_type,
        line_item_unblended_cost
    FROM your_cur_table_name
    CROSS JOIN UNNEST(tags) AS t(key, value)
    WHERE line_item_product_code IN ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
        AND line_item_iam_principal IS NOT NULL
        AND line_item_iam_principal != ''
        AND t.key LIKE 'iamPrincipal/%'
)
SELECT
    tag_name || ': ' || tag_value AS tags,
    line_item_usage_type,
    SUM(line_item_unblended_cost) AS total_cost
FROM iam_principal_costs
GROUP BY tag_name, tag_value, line_item_usage_type
ORDER BY total_cost DESC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="real-world-use-case-multi-service-cost-comparison"&gt;Real-world use case: Multi-service cost comparison&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider a platform team running multiple AI-powered services. For example, the team might run a document summarization pipeline (DocProcessor) and a customer-facing chatbot (ChatApp). The team can assign each service to its own IAM role. With the query patterns from the preceding section, you can isolate how the spend for each service trends with the following query:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
    line_item_iam_principal,
    line_item_usage_type,
    SUM(line_item_usage_amount) AS total_usage,
    SUM(line_item_unblended_cost) AS total_cost
FROM your_cur_table_name
WHERE line_item_product_code IN ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
    AND billing_period = DATE_FORMAT(CURRENT_DATE, '%Y-%m')
    AND line_item_iam_principal IS NOT NULL
    AND (
        line_item_iam_principal LIKE '%DocProcessor%'
        OR line_item_iam_principal LIKE '%ChatApp%'
    )
GROUP BY
    line_item_iam_principal,
    line_item_usage_type
ORDER BY total_cost DESC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Example output:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;line_item_usage_type&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;total_usage&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;total_cost&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/ChatApp/session-1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USE1-Claude4.6Sonnet-output-tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4,800,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$72.00&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/ChatApp/session-1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USE1-Claude4.6Sonnet-input-tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2,900,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$8.70&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/DocProcessor/batch-7&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USE1-NovaLite-output-tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6,100,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$1.46&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/DocProcessor/batch-7&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;USE1-NovaLite-input-tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,200,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0.19&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From this output, the platform team can answer questions like:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Which application is the top contributor to this month’s Bedrock spend?&lt;/strong&gt; In this example, ChatApp accounts for over $80 using Claude 4.6 Sonnet, while DocProcessor costs under $5 using Nova Lite.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Could we reduce costs by using a different model for each workload?&lt;/strong&gt; DocProcessor is already on Nova Lite (appropriate for straightforward summarization tasks), but the team might evaluate whether ChatApp could handle some interactions with a lighter model to reduce the $72 output-token cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cost-of-athena-queries"&gt;Cost of Athena queries&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You pay only for the queries that you run. You are charged based on the amount of data scanned by each query. The console displays this information after each query, and this information is also available in the Recent Queries tab in the Athena console.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Athena queries are billed at $5 per TB scanned (with a 10 MB minimum per query). Because our table automatically uses &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/athena/latest/ug/partition-projection.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;hive partition projection&lt;/a&gt; on billing_period, queries scoped to a single month only scan the parquet files in that month’s folder. Scans are typically well under 10 MB, which costs about $0.00005 per query (the 10 MB minimum).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To keep costs low, always include a &lt;code&gt;WHERE billing_period = ...&lt;/code&gt; filter and select only the columns you need rather than &lt;code&gt;SELECT *&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-cloud-intelligence-dashboards-framework"&gt;The Cloud Intelligence Dashboards framework&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/guidance/latest/cloud-intelligence-dashboards/cudos-cid-kpi.html#foundational-cudos-dashboard" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CUDOS dashboard&lt;/a&gt; is part of the open source &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/guidance/latest/cloud-intelligence-dashboards/getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cloud Intelligence Dashboards (CID) framework&lt;/a&gt;, which you can deploy in your AWS account using the provided infrastructure as code (IaC) templates. The framework helps you drive financial accountability and increase operational efficiency across your AWS organizations. The CUDOS dashboard provides detailed and actionable insights, supporting data-driven decisions for cost efficiency across your AWS infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-bedrock-cost-and-usage-insights-in-cudos"&gt;Amazon Bedrock cost and usage insights in CUDOS&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-solutions-library-samples/cloud-intelligence-dashboards-framework/blob/main/changes/CHANGELOG-cudos.md#cudos---580" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CUDOS version 5.8&lt;/a&gt; introduces a comprehensive Amazon Bedrock section in the AI/ML tab, with full IAM principal cost attribution support. The dashboard provides:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Flexible grouping dimensions:&lt;/strong&gt; Group your Amazon Bedrock spend by IAM Principal, IAM Principal Tags (such as Project or Team), Model/Resource Group, Region, or any other cost taxonomy fields configured during dashboard deployment.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost-per-million-tokens tracking:&lt;/strong&gt; A trend line overlaid on your spend chart showing how cost per million tokens evolves over time, helping you measure the impact of model selection changes or prompt optimization efforts, like caching for example.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure shows the Amazon Bedrock Summary section in the AI/ML tab of the CUDOS dashboard, grouped by IAM principal, with per-principal cost breakdown and cost-per-million-tokens trend.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-21078-3.png" alt="CUDOS dashboard AI/ML tab showing Amazon Bedrock spend grouped by IAM principal with a cost-per-million-tokens trend line" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;CUDOS dashboard showing Amazon Bedrock spend grouped by IAM principal&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Interactive drill-down filtering:&lt;/strong&gt; Choose any value in the top-level spend chart (such as a specific project, principal, or account) and every other visual automatically filters to that selection, letting you drill from a high-level overview into per-model and per-usage-type detail without navigating away from the dashboard.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Granular model and usage breakdown:&lt;/strong&gt; Additional visuals, filtered by the top-level chart, show spend per model, per usage type, and cost-per-million-tokens by model, so you can identify which models and token types are driving costs for a given team or project.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you switch the grouping to IAM Principal Tag Project and choose a specific project (in this example, “chatbot-v2”), all other visuals filter to show only that project’s spend. The visuals break down that spend by model, usage type, and unit cost trends.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-21078-4.png" alt="CUDOS dashboard showing Amazon Bedrock spend for the chatbot-v2 project broken down by model and usage type" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;CUDOS dashboard showing Amazon Bedrock spend grouped by IAM principal tag Project, filtered to the chatbot-v2 project&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With these visuals, you can quickly answer questions like “Which project is driving the most output token costs?”, “Is our chatbot team using cost-efficient models?”, or “How has our cost per million tokens changed since we switched from Opus to Sonnet?”, without writing any SQL.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="getting-started-with-cudos"&gt;Getting started with CUDOS&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started with CUDOS, you can explore the Bedrock section in an &lt;a href="https://cid.workshops.aws.dev/demo/?dashboard=cudos&amp;amp;sheet=AI/ML" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;interactive demo&lt;/a&gt; dashboard. Follow the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/guidance/latest/cloud-intelligence-dashboards/deployment-in-global-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;deployment guide&lt;/a&gt; to set up CUDOS in your organization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you’re already using CUDOS, follow the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/guidance/latest/cloud-intelligence-dashboards/update-dashboards.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;update guidance&lt;/a&gt; to upgrade to version 5.8. You can also use the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/guidance/latest/cloud-intelligence-dashboards/add-org-taxonomy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;add organizational taxonomy&lt;/a&gt; to add IAM Principal data to your existing CUDOS dashboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, drop the Athena table and AWS Glue database (these are metadata, so no compute is running):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Warning: Dropping the Athena table and Glue database will remove your ability to query CUR data. You will need to recreate these resources following the preceding section if you want to analyze billing data in the future.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws glue delete-table --region us-east-1 --database-name your_cur_table_name --name curexport
aws glue delete-database --region us-east-1 --name your_cur_table_name&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Second, if you no longer need the cost data itself, disable the export in the AWS Billing and Cost Management console under Data Exports and empty the S3 prefix it writes to. Note this is your raw billing history, so only delete it if you’re sure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Finally, clear out any Athena query results that have accumulated:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws s3 rm s3://&amp;lt;your-cur-bucket&amp;gt;/athena-results/ --recursive&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are no crawlers, AWS Lambda functions, or schedules to delete. Partition projection means the only ongoing cost is S3 storage for the CUR files themselves, which is typically pennies per month.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For cleaning up CUDOS, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/guidance/latest/cloud-intelligence-dashboards/dashboard-teardown.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CUDOS dashboard teardown instructions&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion-and-next-steps"&gt;Conclusion and next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This two-part series walked through a complete toolkit for understanding and managing Amazon Bedrock inference costs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Part 1&lt;/strong&gt; introduced granular cost attribution: how Amazon Bedrock automatically captures the IAM principal behind every inference call, and how you can use cost allocation tags to aggregate spend by team, project, or tenant.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Part 2&lt;/strong&gt; (this post) showed you how to put that data to work: setting up CUR 2.0 with IAM principal data, querying cost patterns in Amazon Athena, and comparing spend across projects and principals to inform cost allocation decisions. It also introduces the CUDOS dashboard, which provides a comprehensive AI/ML sheet with similar insights for Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Turn on CUR 2.0 with caller identity data in the AWS Billing console, connect it to Athena using the provided agent.md file, and run your first cost-by-principal query. Track Bedrock adoption across your organization with the CUDOS dashboard.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>How OneAdvanced deployed over 50 AI agents on UK-sovereign AWS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-oneadvanced-deployed-over-50-ai-agents-on-uk-sovereign-aws/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Astrid Bowser, Alex Savage Nick Heap]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 12 Aug 2026 13:46:28 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">86cd8b92b90187d96c8febe2b94e065d6f61fea4</guid>

					<description>Learn how OneAdvanced, a UK enterprise software provider, built a UK-sovereign AI platform by self-hosting Llama 4 Maverick and Llama Guard 4 on Amazon SageMaker AI, with a RAG pipeline on pgvector and over 50 agents built with Strands Agents SDK on Amazon ECS.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-authored with OneAdvanced team&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploying AI agents on a United Kingdom (UK)-sovereign AWS architecture requires careful decisions about model hosting, data residency, and agent orchestration. OneAdvanced, a UK-based enterprise software provider serving over 10,000 customers, needed to deliver AI capabilities while making sure that no data would leave the UK. At the time, the specific models they wanted, Llama 4 Maverick and Llama Guard 4, weren’t yet available through managed services in the UK region. OneAdvanced took a different path: self-hosting open-weight large language models (LLMs) on AWS infrastructure they fully control.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we describe how OneAdvanced built a UK-sovereign AI solution using Llama 4 Maverick and Llama Guard 4 on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;. The solution pairs a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline backed by &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/aurora/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL-Compatible Edition&lt;/a&gt; with the &lt;a href="https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;pgvector&lt;/a&gt; extension, over 50 specialized agents powered by &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;, and a tool layer running on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt;. We walk through the architecture, key implementation decisions, and results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-data-sovereignty-and-model-hosting"&gt;The challenge: Data sovereignty and model hosting&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OneAdvanced provides sector-focused software as a service (SaaS) solutions to organizations in healthcare, legal, and many other regulated industries. Their customers handle sensitive data daily, including patient records, legal case files, and compliance documentation. These customers expect that AI tooling meets strict data residency, security, and privacy standards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As Andrew Henderson, CTO of OneAdvanced, explains in the &lt;a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a091czAG2fA" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OneAdvanced AI launch video&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Data sovereignty, particularly in the UK, is a hard requirement for many of our customers, especially those in the public sector and highly regulated industries. They need to know exactly where their data is, who has access to it, and that it resides within the UK’s legal and regulatory framework to support total compliance and trust.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OneAdvanced initially prototyped with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; and saw rapid results within a two-week sprint: chat completion, an Amazon Bedrock Agent for querying UK statute law, Snowflake data integration, and chart generation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;However, to meet their sovereignty requirements, OneAdvanced needed to host models exclusively in their own UK-based AWS accounts. At the time of the engagement, the models they wanted, Llama 4 Maverick and Llama Guard 4, weren’t yet available through AWS managed services in the UK region. Self-hosting meant deploying, serving, and scaling these models while building a production-grade solution around them. This included content moderation, document retrieval, agent orchestration, and a no-code agent builder for non-technical users.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the high-level architecture of the OneAdvanced AI solution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/03/ML-20317-1.png" alt="High-level architecture of the OneAdvanced UK-sovereign AI solution on AWS" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: High-level architecture of the OneAdvanced UK-sovereign AI solution&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This architecture enabled OneAdvanced to achieve full UK data sovereignty and rapid agent deployment, supporting their ISO 42001 certification for AI governance while maintaining complete control over their model serving infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution consists of four components. &lt;a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;vLLM&lt;/a&gt; serves Llama 4 Maverick (FP8) and Llama Guard 4 on Amazon SageMaker AI, running on &lt;code&gt;p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances in the London (&lt;code&gt;eu-west-2&lt;/code&gt;) region. Over 50 Strands agents run on Amazon ECS, each with its own system prompt, tool configuration, and optional input form, with agent configuration stored in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt;. Documents uploaded to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt; are converted to markdown, chunked, and embedded into pgvector for retrieval. Llama Guard 4 checks user inputs for harmful content before the request reaches the main model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A typical request flows like this: the user sends a message, and Llama Guard checks it for harmful content (evaluated before the main inference model). The request routes to the appropriate Strands agent on Amazon ECS. The agent calls tools and retrieves relevant documents from pgvector and Amazon S3 as needed, or invokes specialist tools including web search.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To self-host an AI model on AWS, you need the following resources and skills:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to &lt;code&gt;p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances in your target Region. For information about requesting GPU instance quota, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/regions-quotas.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Intermediate knowledge of deploying containers on Amazon ECS and managing storage with Amazon S3. For getting started, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECS/latest/developerguide/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon ECS Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to your chosen AI model weights (Llama 4 Maverick and Llama Guard 4 require license acceptance on &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/meta-llama" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Experience with Python-based machine learning (ML) frameworks and container orchestration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A PostgreSQL database with the pgvector extension enabled for vector similarity search.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="model-deployment-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai"&gt;Model deployment on Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OneAdvanced serves Llama 4 Maverick (&lt;code&gt;meta-llama/Llama-4-Maverick-17B-128E-Instruct-FP8&lt;/code&gt;) and Llama Guard 4 (&lt;code&gt;meta-llama/Llama-Guard-4-12B&lt;/code&gt;) using vLLM on Amazon SageMaker AI endpoints. They deploy on &lt;code&gt;p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances in the London region, using Hugging Face models with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/machine-learning/containers/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Longer context windows drove the move to P5 instances. OneAdvanced targets 120K–128K token context lengths to support use cases like large document analysis and multi-turn conversations. During the advisory engagement with AWS, load testing with vLLM on P5 instances validated that the infrastructure could handle their throughput requirements. OneAdvanced started on &lt;code&gt;p4d.24xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances and moved to &lt;code&gt;p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; for production, also taking advantage of reserved instance discounts on GPU compute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Llama Guard 4 replaced an earlier deployment of Llama Guard 3 after OneAdvanced observed high false rejection rates with the previous version. The guard model runs serially before the main model, screening user inputs for harmful content before inference begins.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="building-50-agents-with-strands-agents-sdk"&gt;Building over 50 agents with Strands Agents SDK&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A distinctive aspect of the OneAdvanced solution is its agent library: over 50 task-specific agents spanning healthcare, legal, HR, marketing, logistics, and more. Agents include a Care Incident Response assistant, a Clinical Safety Bulletin generator, a scheme of work generator for education, an operational scenario simulation, a performance review assistant, a Document Comparison tool, and an AWS Architect Agent, among many others. OneAdvanced went from their first agent to over 50 in only three weeks, with most agents built in less than a day.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OneAdvanced evaluated several agentic frameworks, including LangChain, LangGraph, and others, before choosing Strands Agents SDK. The decision came down to a few factors: Strands takes a model-first approach with no rigid workflow definitions, it supports turn-taking and interview-style interactions, and it allowed OneAdvanced to move quickly from idea to deployed agent. As Nick Heap, Principal Software Engineer at OneAdvanced, explains:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Post-evaluation, Strands stood out as the clear frontrunner for the project. Its comprehensive suite of tools not only met our requirements but also offered a future-proof solution that closely aligned with our in-house vision.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each agent is defined with a system prompt, a set of tools, and an optional structured input form. Agents are containerized and deployed on Amazon ECS, with runtime configuration stored in Amazon DynamoDB. Users browse the agent catalog and select the one that fits their task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OneAdvanced also built a no-code agent builder that non-developers can use to create and configure agents through a visual interface. Users define the agent’s persona, design input forms with drag-and-drop fields, write system prompts with references to form fields (using an @ syntax to inject form values), and select from a library of available tools. This design makes agent creation accessible to product managers, clinicians, and business analysts without writing code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents draw from a shared tool library that includes calculator, chart creation, file content reader, mermaid diagram generator, organization and personal knowledge search, spreadsheet query (including Snowflake integration), text file query, UK statute law search, and web search (opt-in per organization and per user query). For example, an agent can query a Snowflake database and then generate a chart from the results in a single interaction.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OneAdvanced also uses sub-agents and advanced agentic patterns to maintain the context window across complex interactions. A notable design pattern is the interview-style agent. Some agents, like the Strategic Thinking assistant, ask structured questions before providing advice: “Question 1 of 3: What specific pain points does your AI solution aim to address?” This builds context through turn-taking rather than expecting users to provide everything upfront. Focused, guided interactions produce better outcomes than open-ended prompts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="rag-pipeline"&gt;RAG pipeline&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The RAG system gives agents access to both personal and organization-level document collections. Users upload documents through the file management interface into either a personal space or a shared organization space on Amazon S3. Documents are converted to markdown and chunked into 2,048-token segments before embedding into pgvector for vector similarity search. For longer documents, recursive summarization handles content that exceeds the chunk size.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For embeddings, OneAdvanced uses the &lt;code&gt;intfloat/multilingual-e5-large-instruct&lt;/code&gt; model, chosen for its strong multilingual support and instruction-following capabilities. Rather than adopting an off-the-shelf RAG framework, OneAdvanced built their own retrieval system, internally called “Llamadex,” giving them full control over the pipeline. They initially chose pgvector for its simplicity and the speed it offered in getting to production. As the solution matures, OneAdvanced is reviewing this strategy to evaluate alternatives.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When an agent needs to answer a question grounded in uploaded documents, it calls the appropriate retrieval tool, “Personal knowledge search” or “Organization knowledge search,” which queries pgvector and returns relevant chunks along with source references. Users can see exactly which documents were used to generate a response, supporting transparency and trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-responsible-ai"&gt;Security and responsible AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Security runs through every layer of the OneAdvanced AI solution, a direct consequence of serving customers who handle sensitive data daily in sectors like health and legal.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The infrastructure runs in the London AWS Region. No user data leaves the UK. No user queries or responses are retained or used for model training. As Nick Heap wrote:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“An AI service that is only hosted in the UK and data is not used to train the AI model and OneAdvanced engineers do not have access to the data.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Uploaded documents in personal and organization spaces are fully isolated and not readable even by OneAdvanced staff. Llama Guard 4 screens user inputs for harmful content before they reach the main model. Privacy controls are customizable at organizational levels. On the infrastructure side, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/guardduty/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon GuardDuty&lt;/a&gt; provides threat detection on the Amazon S3 document storage layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OneAdvanced holds ISO 42001 certification for AI governance—a certification they report being among the first organizations in the UK and Europe to achieve. They’re also a signatory to the EU AI Pact, reflecting a proactive approach to responsible AI across their products and operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Through the AWS advisory engagement, OneAdvanced went from prototype to production on a UK-sovereign AI solution. OneAdvanced deployed Llama 4 Maverick on P5 instances in the London region, giving them full control over model serving infrastructure with no data leaving the UK. They built and shipped over 50 Strands agents in only three weeks, covering use cases across healthcare, legal, HR, marketing, and more. The solution has been running in production since July 2025, serving customers for over a year. It met its target performance metrics. OneAdvanced publicly launched this as the UK’s first private sovereign AI for business, with no user data retained, trained on, or logged. The engagement received a 5/5 customer satisfaction score.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“We really appreciate and enjoyed the engagement. We learned a lot which helped us launch a unique offering to the market powered and supported by our strategic partner. Thank you very much to all involved directly and indirectly.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Alex Savage, Head of Integration, OneAdvanced&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion-and-next-steps"&gt;Conclusion and next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how OneAdvanced built a UK-sovereign AI solution by self-hosting Llama 4 Maverick and Llama Guard 4 on Amazon SageMaker AI, orchestrating over 50 specialized agents with Strands Agents SDK on Amazon ECS, and grounding responses in customer documents through a RAG pipeline backed by pgvector. The result is a production AI solution that meets strict data sovereignty requirements while delivering practical AI capabilities to enterprise customers in regulated industries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OneAdvanced has recently implemented an evaluation framework using LLM-as-a-judge with sentiment analysis for continuous quality improvement. Their roadmap also includes backend systems to help users refine their prompts, and running parallel LLM models during version transitions to maintain stability for end users.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you’re considering a similar approach, start by evaluating your data sovereignty requirements and identifying which models you need to self-host. OneAdvanced began with a two-week prototype on Amazon Bedrock to validate their use cases before committing to self-hosted infrastructure. For more information about deploying models on Amazon SageMaker AI, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/whatis.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;. To get started with agentic workflows, visit the &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt; on GitHub.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To read more about OneAdvanced’s journey building their AI solution, see Nick Heap’s post &lt;a href="https://careers.oneadvanced.com/employee-stories/the_ai_race_how_we_fully_embraced_velocity/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;The AI race and how we fully embraced velocity&lt;/a&gt; on the OneAdvanced careers blog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you have a similar use case and would like to explore sovereign AI deployment on AWS, contact your &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/contact-us/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account team&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Pay with confidence: How Solv Labs built verifiable, auditable agent payments on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/pay-with-confidence-how-solv-labs-built-verifiable-auditable-agent-payments-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-payments/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Patrick Duffy]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 12 Aug 2026 13:44:58 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">c7f6cc41ecaa8180ee996e7324c0b62d83cf69cb</guid>

					<description>Solv Labs built a governed agent-payments workflow on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments, where every transaction is authorized, attested in an AWS Nitro Enclave, priced for risk, and anchored to a public blockchain before settlement. See how the pattern gives enterprises a verifiable, auditable trail for autonomous agent payments in regulated environments.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Patrick Duffy from Solv Labs and Houman Shadab from ICME Labs&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Solv Labs built an AI agent-payments workflow using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, governed by two layers: ORACLE (Solv’s policy engine) and ICME PreFlight for compliance verification. AgentCore payments provides the payment processing infrastructure. ORACLE enforces authorization policies before each transaction. ICME’s verification layer extends AWS Automated Reasoning Checks to make them privacy-preserving, portable, and independently verifiable, with each decision checkable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As a result, every agent payment runs through three core governance components: ORACLE for pre-authorization decisions, an integrity service running in an AWS Nitro Enclave, and a risk engine for per-transaction pricing. Each transaction completes in under four seconds, covering pre-authorization, governance, and on-chain settlement through Coinbase. Every transaction produces a full audit trail, and that’s well within latency budgets for agentic workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first time an autonomous agent moves real money on behalf of an enterprise, the question is no longer “Did it work?” It is “Can we prove what just happened?” In a single quarter, four pieces of infrastructure that make that question answerable arrived together: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments, AWS Automated Reasoning Checks, AWS Nitro Enclaves used as a per-transaction attester, and the x402 payment standard for agent payments to services.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon introduced Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments in May 2026, built in partnership with Coinbase and Stripe. It lets AI agents instantly access and pay for what they use, including web content, APIs, MCP servers, and other agents. Spending is governed by the same controls developers already use to operate their agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, Solv Labs and policy-verification partner ICME walk through how we built an agent-payments workflow on AgentCore payments where every transaction is governed at execution time, attested inside an AWS Nitro Enclave, risk-priced individually, and fully auditable. We also cover what this pattern unlocks for enterprises running agents in regulated environments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-enterprise-challenge"&gt;The enterprise challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When an autonomous system moves money, the operator must prove to auditors, counterparties, and legal that each payment was authorized, priced for the risk it carried, and recorded in a way that holds up to scrutiny. A misconfigured or manipulated agent doesn’t only return a bad answer. It moves money. And the hard part isn’t executing the payment. It’s proving, afterward, that the payment was permitted in the first place.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enterprise teams consistently struggle with the same gap: when an agent transaction completes, there’s no durable record tying that specific action to the policy that authorized it, the constraints it satisfied, or the risk it carried. Model cards, SOC 2 reports, and after-the-fact reviews describe the &lt;em&gt;organization&lt;/em&gt; around the system. They don’t describe the &lt;em&gt;execution&lt;/em&gt; of individual decisions. Without that transaction-level binding, operators have no clean way to resolve a dispute, satisfy an auditor, or tell a routine agent action apart from a compromised one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Solv Labs needed every agent action to be verifiable, risk-priced, and auditable on demand, without slowing agents down or bolting on infrastructure outside the system they already run on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="vision"&gt;Vision&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The vision is straightforward: govern every agent payment at execution time and produce a record that various parties (auditors, counterparties, regulators) can verify independently without depending on the operator’s word for it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This became practical only recently, with AgentCore payments as the payment orchestration layer, Automated Reasoning Checks (ARc) for formal policy evaluation, Nitro Enclaves for hardware attestation, and x402 reaching broad adoption for agent payments. Together, they made governed agent payments an implementation choice rather than a research program.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The workflow runs as a set of specialized components: AgentCore payments in a Solv-operated environment, the ORACLE engine with its Nitro Enclave attester, and policy checks from ICME as an external service. Components only cross trust boundaries through signed, hash-bound artifacts, so the evidence flow holds regardless of how the services are deployed. The workflow attaches to an agent built on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and is compatible with agents running on AgentCore runtime or custom AgentCore implementations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21446-1.png" alt="System architecture diagram showing an agent payment flowing through pre-authorization, constraint verification, integrity attestation, and risk pricing before settlement" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: System architecture. Every agent payment passes through pre-authorization, constraint verification, integrity attestation, and risk pricing before settlement, all native to AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every payment runs through five specialized components, each addressing a distinct part of the governance problem.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ORACLE – pre-authorization.&lt;/strong&gt; ORACLE evaluates the proposed action against the applicable policy and returns an ALLOW or REVIEW determination before values move, so a policy failure doesn’t produce a settled transaction the operator has to unwind.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;PreFlight – independently verifiable policy checks.&lt;/strong&gt; ICME’s PreFlight provides the policy check underlying ORACLE’s decision. It produces a small, privacy-preserving proof of that check that a third party can verify without access to the policy or the transaction parameters.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Nitro Enclave – integrity attestation.&lt;/strong&gt; An integrity service running in a Nitro Enclave signs the execution record inside a hardware-isolated environment. The attestation document, produced by the Nitro Security Module, binds the signing key to the specific enclave image measurements (PCR0, with PCR1 and PCR2 also included). As a result, a verifier can confirm not just that the record was signed inside an enclave, but inside the specific enclave image Solv Labs has published. Each action is therefore cryptographically attested and bound to the enclave that produced it, so the record can’t be silently rewritten after the fact.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Risk engine – per-transaction pricing.&lt;/strong&gt; The risk engine attaches a risk multiplier to each transaction, computed deterministically from the assessed violation signal, so the governance record carries a risk price rather than a flat pass. The multiplier informs downstream review prioritization and, where applicable, pricing of risk transferred to a third party.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Payment processing and settlement.&lt;/strong&gt; AgentCore payments processes each payment while enforcing per-session spending limits, keeping the transaction within the budget the end user has authorized. Settlement then completes with on-chain routing through Coinbase.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These components run in a fixed order: ORACLE’s decision, its independently verifiable proof, the hardware attestation, and the per-transaction risk price are all produced before settlement is initiated. The gate is absolute: no decision, no settlement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21446-2.png" alt="Transaction sequence diagram showing steps 1 to 3 producing the governance record before settlement is initiated" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Transaction sequence. Steps 1–3 produce the governance record before settlement is initiated, and the gate enforces “no decision, no settlement.” End-to-end latency is under four seconds per transaction, with governance overhead under one second&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-the-governance-layer-attests"&gt;What the governance layer attests&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each governed payment produces a single signed evidence record binding five things: the policy that was evaluated, the policy-check result and its independently verifiable proof, the hardware-attested execution record, the per-transaction risk price, and the settlement artifacts from AgentCore payments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It doesn’t attest that the agent’s underlying decision was wise, that the counterparty is solvent, or that the policy itself is correct. Those remain the operator’s responsibility, as for other payments. What it &lt;em&gt;does&lt;/em&gt; attest, in a way parties can verify, is that this specific payment was evaluated against this specific policy under these specific constraints at this specific risk price, and that the result of that evaluation is what authorized settlement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21446-3.png" alt="Per-transaction evidence record fields including execution and policy hashes, constraint result, proof reference, attestation digests, risk multiplier, and on-chain anchor" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Per-transaction evidence record containing execution and policy hashes, constraint result, zero-knowledge proof reference, hardware attestation digests, risk multiplier, and on-chain anchor. Hashes and identifiers are illustrative placeholders. The full record is canonicalized, Ed25519-signed inside the Nitro Enclave, and anchored on-chain&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-agentcore-payments-closes-the-governance-gap"&gt;How AgentCore payments closes the governance gap&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three properties of the service make the workflow operationally clean:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Native to the agent system.&lt;/strong&gt; Running inside the same AgentCore environment as the agent, the governance workflow inherits the agent’s identity, gateway, and observability surfaces. Operators don’t run two parallel control planes, which is the configuration where most governance gaps appear in practice.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Infrastructure-level spending limits.&lt;/strong&gt; AgentCore payments enforces per-session spending limits independently of anything the agent or policy engine decides. Defense in depth is built in, not assembled.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A single observability surface.&lt;/strong&gt; Every decision, attestation, risk price, and settlement is visible through standard AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, on Amazon CloudWatch logs, metrics, and traces, alongside everything else the agent does.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="customer-perspective-solv-labs"&gt;Customer perspective: Solv Labs&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Before we built this workflow, the thing we couldn’t give an enterprise was a clean answer to why a specific payment was permitted — only assertions about the organization that authorized it. AgentCore payments let us move that answer to the transaction itself: every payment now carries the policy it cleared, the enclave that signed it, and the proof a third party can check. The evidence travels with the transaction.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Patrick Duffy, CEO, Solv Labs&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="customer-perspective-icme"&gt;Customer perspective: ICME&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“When the verifier of a payment decision isn’t the operator who made it, you need a way to prove the check ran correctly without exposing the policy itself. That’s what ICME adds to AWS Automated Reasoning Checks: every decision comes with a cryptographic proof a counterparty or regulator can verify in under a second without seeing the policy detail or the transaction parameters.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Houman Shadab, Co-Founder, ICME Labs&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The workflow produces a per-transaction, hardware-attested, independently verifiable governance record before settlement of a payment transaction by an agent. Enterprises adopting this pattern on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments get the following, per payment transaction, at machine speed:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Verifiable execution on transactions:&lt;/strong&gt; Each agent action is cryptographically signed and independently verifiable against a public anchor on the Base networks. Governed payments processed across both decision paths, ALLOW and REVIEW, and both carry the same evidence guarantee. The DENY path, fully implemented and unit-tested in the ORACLE engine, produces a signed refusal record when configured constraints are violated.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Risk pricing:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Each transaction carries a deterministically computed risk multiplier, so the governance record prices the risk it carried rather than treating every payment as identical. The risk multipliers reflect the engine’s operating point, with outcome calibration accruing as observed executions accumulate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Independent auditability:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Each governing decision is anchored on a public blockchain, designed for third-party verification using reference verifier tooling from Solv Labs and ICME, without exposing the policy detail, transaction parameters, or private keys.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Governance at machine speed:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Transactions complete in under four seconds, covering pre-authorization, governance, payment processing, and on-chain settlement through Coinbase. Governance-call latency holds within the latency budget of the overall transaction.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A single, observable surface:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Each decision, attestation, and risk price is visible through the same AgentCore Observability on Amazon CloudWatch logs, metrics, and traces operators use for the rest of agent behavior. The record is structured for the people who consume it: Risk and Compliance, Internal Audit, and external auditors and counterparties, each able to verify.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21446-4.png" alt="The per-transaction evidence stack showing four artifacts produced before settlement and recorded together for independent verification" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: The per-transaction evidence stack. All four artifacts are produced before settlement fires and recorded together, and auditors, counterparties, and regulators can verify each element independently without access to the operator’s policy detail, transaction parameters, or private keys&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-this-unlocks-for-enterprises"&gt;What this unlocks for enterprises&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A clean audit trail per transaction&lt;/strong&gt;, produced at the moment of execution rather than reconstructed afterward: what was authorized, under which policy, against which constraints, at what risk price, signed by which enclave, and anchored where.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evidence that survives a dispute.&lt;/strong&gt; When a counterparty, auditor, or regulator asks why a payment was permitted, the answer is a verifiable artifact, not an organizational assertion. This matters most in regulated environments, where evidence-retention obligations attach to each transaction.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Governance at agent speed.&lt;/strong&gt; Sub-second governance overhead means enterprises get both control and the throughput agentic workloads require.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Review effort that scales with exceptions, not volume.&lt;/strong&gt; Because every transaction carries its own evidence and clears a policy gate before settlement, review shifts from sampling every Nth transaction to investigating the exceptions the evidence itself flags. Oversight work grows with the exception rate, not the transaction rate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A control cost that fits the budget line you already own.&lt;/strong&gt; Running on AgentCore’s native surfaces, there is no parallel control plane to license, integrate, or staff. The marginal cost of governing one more payment is dominated by the AgentCore call itself, with proof and anchoring as marginal cost on top of a line enterprises already pay, not a new line item.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A foundation for the workloads ahead.&lt;/strong&gt; The same per-transaction evidence model scales to the volume and diversity of payment actions agentic workloads will generate in production.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments together with ORACLE, PreFlight and an AWS Nitro Enclave attester, Solv Labs built a governed agent-payments workflow in which every transaction is evaluated against policy, attested in hardware, priced for risk, and anchored to a public blockchain, all before settlement fires. For enterprises, that means review effort that scales with exceptions rather than volume, a control cost that fits a budget line they already own, and an audit trail their Risk, Compliance, and Audit functions can each verify without access to the operator’s policy detail, transaction parameters, or private keys. That is what it takes to deploy agent payments in regulated environments without trading speed for control.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/technical-deep-dive-agentcore-payments-and-innovation-in-agentic-commerce/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments&lt;/a&gt;, see the Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments documentation&lt;/a&gt;. To explore Solv Labs’ governed agent payments, visit &lt;a href="https://www.solvrisk.xyz/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Solv Labs&lt;/a&gt;. To learn more about ICME’s PreFlight, visit &lt;a href="https://icme.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ICME&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
